What if 11 - 6 = 5. Then we have 5, 5, 1 left. Then 5 * 5 - 1 = 25 - 1 = 24. That works! Let's check: If we take 11 - 6 = 5, and then we already have a 5, and then 5 * 5 - 1 = 25 - 1 = 24. But then we used 11 and 6 to get 5, and then we have the original 5 and 1. But wait, we must use each number exactly once. We have numbers: 1, 5, 6, 11. In the expression 5 * 5 - 1, we are using 5 twice and 1 once. But we can get one of the 5's as (11 - 6). So then the numbers used are: (11 - 6) gives 5, then the other 5 is the original 5, and then 1 is the original 1. And 6 and 11 are used in (11 - 6). So the expression is: (11 - 6) * 5 - 1. Let's check: (11 - 6) = 5, then 5 * 5 = 25, then 25 - 1 = 24. And we used 11, 6, 5, 1 exactly once. The operations used are subtraction, multiplication, and subtraction. And all are allowed. So the expression is: (11 - 6) * 5 - 1. That equals 5 * 5 - 1 = 25 - 1 = 24. That is a valid solution.

Let's double-check: Numbers: 11, 6, 5, 1. Expression: (11 - 6) * 5 - 1. Calculation: 11 - 6 = 5, then 5 * 5 = 25, then 25 - 1 = 24.

That works perfectly.

Thus final answer: \boxed{(11-6)\times5-1}.

I'll now provide the final answer in the required format: \boxed{(11-6)\times5-1}.</think>We can notice that if we form 5 from 11 and 6 by subtracting (11 – 6), then we have the numbers 5 and 1 left. In fact,

  (11 – 6) = 5

so then

  5 × 5 – 1 = 25 – 1 = 24.

This uses 11, 6, 5, and 1 exactly once (the 5 coming from 11–6 and the other 5 as given) and only the allowed operations.

Thus, one valid answer is

\boxed{(11-6)\times5-1}
